Measurements of charged-particle pseudorapidity and transverse momentum distributions in O+O and Ne+Ne collisions at s NN = 5.36 \sqrt{s_{{}_{\text{NN}}}}=5.36 TeV with the ATLAS detector


Abstract
Measurements of charged-particle transverse momentum spectra, multiplicity, and mean transverse momentum are presented as a function of pseudorapidity and collision centrality in O+O and Ne+Ne collisions at s NN = 5.36 \sqrt{s_{{}_{\text{NN}}}}=5.36 TeV using 27.7 ​ μ ​ b − 1 27.7\penalty\ \mu\text{b}^{-1} and 53.1 ​ μ ​ b − 1 53.1\penalty\ \mu\text{b}^{-1} data sets recorded by the ATLAS experiment at the LHC. The collision centrality is characterized by the total transverse energy measured in the ATLAS forward calorimeters. The kinematics of charged particles are reconstructed with the inner detector over the fiducial pseudorapidity and transverse momentum ranges | η | < 2.5 |\eta|<2.5 and 0.27 < p T < 5 0.27<p_{\text{T}}<5 GeV using data from the ATLAS inner detector. The per-event charged-particle pseudorapidity density d ​ n / d ​ η dn/d\eta and mean transverse momentum ⟨ p T ⟩ \langle p_{\text{T}}\rangle are measured over this fiducial range as a function of η \eta . The results are reported in 5%-wide centrality intervals covering the 5–80% centrality range, and in 1%-wide intervals covering the 0–5% centrality range. Invariant per-event yields are evaluated as a function of η \eta and p T p_{\text{T}} . Their p T p_{\text{T}} dependence is fitted to estimate extrapolated d ​ n / d ​ η dn/d\eta and ⟨ p T ⟩ \langle p_{\text{T}}\rangle values over 0 < p T < 5 0<p_{\text{T}}<5 GeV. To evaluate the impact of using pseudorapidity instead of rapidity, measurements are also performed as a function of rapidity computed using a pion mass hypothesis. The fiducial and extrapolated results are compared with hydrodynamic calculations. Ratios of the p T p_{\text{T}} -differential yields between centrality intervals, and between O+O and Ne+Ne collisions, are used to assess the centrality- and system-dependence of radial flow developed in the collisions.

HION-2025-04

\PreprintIdNumber
CERN-EP-2026-154

\AtlasJournal
Phys. Rev. C.

1
Introduction

Heavy-ion collisions at the Large Hadron Collider (LHC) are understood
to create highly out-of-equilibrium strongly interacting matter at
energy densities well in excess of
10\penalty\ \text{GeV/fm}^{3}

(see Ref.
[
1
]
).
This matter subsequently evolves within
{\lesssim}1
fm/
c

[
2
]

into a “hydrodynamized”, although not yet completely thermalized,
quark–gluon plasma (QGP)
[
3
,
4
]
.
Viscous hydrodynamic calculations
[
5
,
6
]

have been very successful in describing
a variety of soft single- and multi-hadron measurements at the
Relativistic Heavy Ion Collider (RHIC) and the LHC, but only if the
ratio of shear viscosity to entropy density (
\eta/s
)
in units of
\hbar
is
\sim
0.1–0.2, close to the conjectured
lower bound on
\eta/s

[
7
,
8
,
9
,
8
]
.
Such small values require the mean free path of the microscopic
constituents of the plasma
to be much smaller than the transverse size of the plasma which is
determined by the size of the colliding nuclei.

Extensive theoretical advances in the last two decades (see
e.g. Refs.
[
2
,
10
]
)
have provided a sound footing for understanding the rapid
transition from the out-of-equilibrium initial state to the
hydrodynamically evolving plasma.
The results of these advances also provide a rigorous connection between
certain final-state observables and the initial state.
In particular, the density per unit rapidity and per unit transverse area of
charged particles produced in a heavy-ion collision is found to be
closely related to the initial energy density and to the entropy
density in the plasma at the hydrodynamization time
[
11
]
.
Such a connection was originally proposed by Bjorken in the context of
a specific hydrodynamic model
[
12
]
, but it is found to apply even for
the strongly out-of-equilibrium initial conditions of ultrarelativistic
heavy-ion collisions
[
13
,
11
,
14
]
.
In addition, the mean transverse momentum of the produced particles
is sensitive to the collective transverse velocity or “radial flow”
developed during the hydrodynamic expansion
[
15
,
16
]
.

A topic of great interest in the study of the QGP is whether there exists
some transverse size scale below which the above-described paradigm
for the creation, hydrodynamization, and subsequent evolution of
the QGP becomes invalid.
This question is made particularly urgent by the appearance of trends in
single- and multi-hadron data obtained from proton–proton
[
17
,
18
,
19
]
and
proton–nucleus collisions
[
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
that are nearly identical to those observed
in heavy-ion collisions
[
24
,
25
]
, suggesting that QGP can be created with a transverse
size comparable to the size of a proton, i.e. comparable to the QCD
confinement distance scale.
Systematic studies of multiple observables in collisions of nuclei of
different size – since the nuclear size also determines the maximum transverse
size of the plasma – may provide important insight into whether the paradigm
described above for the creation and evolution of the QGP evolves
smoothly with transverse size down to the size of a proton.
Considerations such as this have motivated interest in studying collisions
of nuclei much smaller than lead nuclei at the LHC
[
26
]
.

In July of 2025, the LHC carried out a long-anticipated set of special
runs that included
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions at a center-of-mass energy
of 5.36
TeV
per nucleon pair.
The inclusion of
Ne
+
Ne
data-taking in the special runs arose from recent
strong interest in the heavy-ion community in studying the impact of the
shape of the colliding nuclei on the initial state, the subsequent
hydrodynamic evolution of the QGP, and on observables sensitive to
that evolution
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
.
The
{}^{16}{}\mathrm{O}
and
{}^{20}{}\mathrm{Ne}
nuclei have similar sizes, but are expected to have different
shapes, due to the role of alpha clustering
[
30
]
in
the ground-state wave functions.
In particular, the
{}^{20}{}\mathrm{Ne}
nucleus is thought to acquire a “bowling pin” shape
due to the presence of an additional alpha cluster compared to the

{}^{16}{}\mathrm{O}
nucleus.
Hydrodynamic calculations
[
31
]
that start from

ab initio
calculations of the nuclear
geometry
[
32
,
30
,
33
,
34
]
and
use the Trento model
[
35
]
to describe the initial
particle production and the energy-density distribution at
the onset of hydrodynamic evolution, predict measurable
effects from nuclear-shape differences.
Observables such as the
mean transverse momentum (
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
) and the Fourier coefficients
of the azimuthal harmonics
[
36
]
(
v_{n}
), especially
v_{2}
, show sensitivity
to the different geometries of the
{}^{16}{}\mathrm{O}
and
{}^{20}{}\mathrm{Ne}
nuclei.
Measurements of the azimuthal Fourier coefficients of charged particles
produced in
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions
[
37
,
38
,
39
]

have yielded results consistent with predictions arising from the
hydrodynamic calculations.
However, tests of other predictions from the model, including the charged-particle
pseudorapidity density and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
, are needed to verify both the initial
conditions of the calculations and their description of the radial
flow developed during the evolution of the plasma.

This paper presents measurements of charged-particle pseudorapidity
density, transverse momentum, and mean transverse
momentum in
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions, using data
collected by ATLAS during the aforementioned special runs of the LHC.
The centrality of the
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions is characterized by
the total transverse energy measured in the ATLAS forward calorimeters,
and results are presented over the centrality range 0–80% in both collision systems.
Charged particles are reconstructed from hits in the ATLAS inner
detector over the “fiducial” pseudorapidity
and transverse momentum intervals,
|\eta|<2.5
and
0.27<p_{\text{T}}<5

GeV
, respectively.
The measurements are performed as a function of
\eta
and
rapidity, where the latter is computed assuming the charged-pion
mass hypothesis and denoted by
y_{\pi}
.
The use of pion rapidity provides results that are closer to those obtained using
true rapidity, which is the natural variable for describing
particle production in hadronic interactions but is not accessible
to ATLAS due to its limited ability to identify hadrons.
Results are presented for
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
,
1
1

1

Throughout
this paper, when discussing differential distributions of particle yields
(versus
p_{\text{T}}
or
\eta
) the lower case “
n
” denotes yield per event.
Thus, e.g.

\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta\equiv(1/N_{\text{evt}})\mathrm{d}N/\mathrm{d}\eta
.

\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
, and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
evaluated as a function of
\eta
and
y_{\pi}
. Results are also presented for the double-differential
distributions,
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
and
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
in
different
\eta
or
y_{\pi}
intervals.
Results for
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
are obtained both in the fiducial kinematic
range and with extrapolation to
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
using fits to the
p_{\text{T}}
dependence of
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
and
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
.
The measured
p_{\text{T}}
spectra are compared
between different centrality intervals within the same collision
system and between
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions. The results of the
presented measurements are compared with predictions from hydrodynamic
calculations.

The remainder of this paper is structured as follows:
Section
2
describes the ATLAS experiment;
Section
3
describes the data sets used in the
analysis and the object reconstruction; Section
4

presents the analysis methods; Section
5

discusses systematic uncertainties; Section
6
presents
a subset of the results; and Section
7
summarizes the paper
and discusses conclusions drawn from the measurements. The appendix
presents more results that could not be included in
Section
6
without detracting from its readability.

2
ATLAS detector

The ATLAS experiment
[
40
,
41
]
at the LHC comprises
a nearly hermetic central detector surrounding the collision
point
2
2

2

ATLAS uses a right-handed coordinate system with
its origin at the nominal interaction point (IP) in the center of
the detector and the
z
-axis along the beam pipe.
The
x
-axis points from the IP to the center of the LHC ring,
and the
y
-axis points upwards.
Polar coordinates
(r,\phi)
are used in the transverse plane,

\phi
being the azimuthal angle around the
z
-axis.
The pseudorapidity is defined in terms of the polar angle
\theta

as
\eta=-\ln\tan(\theta/2)
and is equal to the rapidity

y=(1/2)\ln[(E+p_{z})/(E-p_{z})]

in the relativistic limit.
Angular distance is measured in units of
\Delta R\equiv\sqrt{(\Delta y)^{2}+(\Delta\phi)^{2}}
.

and special-purpose forward detectors including the zero-degree
calorimeters (ZDCs) that are described below.
The central detector consists of an inner tracking detector surrounded
by a thin superconducting solenoid, electromagnetic and hadronic
calorimeters covering the region
|\eta|<3.2
and forward
calorimeters (FCals) covering
3.2<|\eta|<4.9
, and a muon spectrometer
incorporating three large superconducting air-core toroidal magnets.

The inner-detector system (ID) is immersed in a
2\text{\,}\mathrm{T}
axial
magnetic field and provides charged-particle tracking in the range

|\eta|<2.5
.
A high-granularity silicon pixel detector covers the vertex region
and typically provides four measurements per track,
the first hit generally being in the insertable B-layer (IBL)
installed before Run 2
[
42
,
43
]
.
It is surrounded by a silicon microstrip tracker (the SCT), which usually
provides eight measurements per track.
These silicon detectors are complemented by a transition radiation
tracker (TRT), which enables radially extended track reconstruction
up to
|\eta|=2.0
.

The FCals
[
44
]
measure transverse energy in the
forward region and are used to characterize the centrality of
heavy-ion collisions.
Each FCal consists of three modules longitudinal in shower depth, one electromagnetic
and two hadronic, with dense absorber matrices and narrow
liquid-argon gaps, optimized to measure the large particle flux
in the forward region.

Two ZDCs
[
45
]
are located at

\Delta z=\pm 140
m from the center of ATLAS,
within the LHC “Target Absorbers for Neutrals” (TANs) shielding components.
The ZDCs measure neutral particles produced with
|\eta|\gtrsim 8.4
.
In heavy-ion collisions, these are primarily spectator neutrons that
did not participate in the collision and were emitted at very
forward rapidities.

The luminosity is measured mainly by the LUCID-2
[
46
]
detector,
which records Cherenkov light produced in the quartz windows of
photomultipliers located close to the beam pipe.

ATLAS uses a two-level trigger system
[
47
]
consisting of
a Level-1 (L1) trigger that is implemented using dedicated electronics
and a software-based “high-level trigger” (HLT) running algorithms
similar to those used in offline analysis.
The Athena software suite
[
48
]
is used in data simulation,
in the reconstruction and analysis of real and simulated data,
in detector operations, and in the trigger and data acquisition
systems of the experiment.

3
Data sets and object reconstruction

The
O
+
O
data used in this analysis were collected during a dedicated period of
low-pileup operation, where the mean number of interactions
per bunch crossing was
\langle\mu\rangle\sim 0.01
.
The
Ne
+
Ne
data were collected in a single LHC fill with

\langle\mu\rangle
varying between 0.04 and 0.16.
Minimum-bias events were selected using an L1 trigger based on the
TRT “FastOR” algorithm
[
49
]
,
together with an HLT requirement of at least one reconstructed
charged-particle track with
p_{\text{T}}>200

MeV
.
The events used in the analysis were recorded during stable LHC
running conditions and have no detector hardware or
readout errors
[
50
]
.
The integrated luminosities of
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions sampled
by the TRT trigger and passing event-cleaning requirements (described
below) are
27.7\penalty\ \mu\text{b}^{-1}
and
53.1\penalty\ \mu\text{b}^{-1}
, respectively.

Charged-particle tracks and collision vertices are reconstructed from
hits in the ID using standard methods
[
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
]
.
For the nominal analysis, the reconstructed tracks are required to
have
|\eta|<2.5
and at least one pixel hit,
with an additional requirement of a hit in the IBL or a hit in the
next-to-innermost pixel layer when one is expected.
3
3

3

A hit is expected if the extrapolated track crosses an active
region of a silicon-sensor module (pixel or SCT) that has not
been disabled.
A hit is said to be “missing” when it is expected but not found.
A missing hit is called a hole.
The hole counting does not consider layers before the first hit
or after the last hit.
If a track crosses a disabled module, then for the
purposes of hit counting, the disabled module is counted as a hit.
If a hit is used in reconstructing more than one track trajectory,
it is called a “shared hit”.

The tracks are required to have at least six SCT hits.
To suppress secondary-track contributions, the transverse impact parameter of the track with
respect to the beamline,
d_{0}
, and the longitudinal impact parameter
of the track relative to the primary vertex
[
52
,
53
]
,

z_{0}\sin(\theta)
, are required to satisfy

|d_{0}|<1.5
mm and
|z_{0}\sin(\theta)|<1.5
mm.
An alternative set of track selections is used to evaluate
systematic uncertainties in the measurement.
Like the nominal selection, the alternative selection requires

|\eta|<2.5
,
|d_{0}|<1.5
mm, and
|z_{0}\sin(\theta)|<1.5
mm,
but its hit requirements are markedly different.
The track is required to have at least eight silicon-detector hits and at most
two silicon-detector holes, with at most one hole in the pixel detector,
at most one shared pixel hit or at most two shared SCT hits.
If a track has shared hits, then the number of required silicon-detector hits
is increased to ten.

The
z
-position of the primary vertex
[
52
,
53
]
is required to lie within
10 cm of the center of ATLAS.
In
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions, backgrounds from ultraperipheral collisions (UPCs)
are suppressed relative to
Pb
+
Pb
collisions, but they still occur at an observable rate.
The dominant UPC background in
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions is
inclusive photonuclear (
\gamma+A
) scattering,
which produces asymmetric events with little energy emitted
in the hemisphere of the photon-emitting nucleus.
A selection that requires at least one topocluster
[
55
]
having
p_{\text{T}}>400

MeV
within the acceptance of each FCal is used to reject these events.
An additional requirement rejects events in which the
fraction of all reconstructed tracks that satisfy the aforementioned
nominal selections is less than 50%.
Such events are mostly associated with upstream interactions
and collisions involving “satellite” ions.
In the recorded
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collision data, there are contributions
from pileup events, in which two or more inelastic collisions
occur in the same bunch crossing.
Most of the pileup events are removed by requiring exactly one
high-quality reconstructed vertex per event.
A high-quality vertex is defined as one that is well-constrained in position, having
a
z
-position variance of less than
0.02\penalty\ \mathrm{mm}^{2}
.
Pileup events are further suppressed by exploiting the correlation
between energy recorded in the FCal (
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}^{\text{FCAL}}
) and the energy
deposited in the ZDCs.
The residual pileup after these selections is estimated to be
negligible.

As in earlier ATLAS studies of heavy-ion collisions, events are categorized
into centrality percentiles based on the

\Sigma E_{\text{T}}^{\text{FCAL}}

[
37
,
56
,
57
,
58
]
.
To relate the
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}^{\text{FCAL}}
distribution to the sampled fraction of the
total inelastic
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
cross-sections,
a Glauber-model-based calculation
[
59
,
60
]

is used to fit the data, to extract the fraction of events selected
above a minimum
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}^{\text{FCAL}}
threshold, and to estimate the systematic
uncertainty of that fraction
[
56
,
57
,
58
]
.
Additionally, the Glauber model is used to extract primary collision
characteristics, such as the average number of participating nucleons,

\langle N_{\mathrm{part}}\rangle
, for each centrality interval.
The
\langle N_{\mathrm{part}}\rangle
values and their uncertainties corresponding to the
centrality intervals used here are shown in Table
1

in the Appendix.
The Glauber analysis used in this paper represents an evolution
of that used in the ATLAS measurement of collective flow in

O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions
[
37
]
.
It explicitly accounts for potential
\alpha
-particle clustering in

{}^{16}{}\mathrm{O}
and
{}^{20}{}\mathrm{Ne}
nuclei by using nucleon density distributions obtained from
the PGCM
[
33
,
30
]
and
NLEFT
[
61
]
initial-state models as implemented in the
Trajectum hydrodynamic simulation code
[
62
]
(
TR_OXYGEN_V15

and
TR_NEON_V14
with
\omega=0.3
).
The
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}^{\text{FCAL}}
values corresponding
to boundaries of
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
centrality intervals have changed
relative to Ref.
[
37
]
by
less than 1% in the most central collisions and as much as
5% in the most peripheral collisions.

The detector performance is studied using a sample of 50 million
minimum-bias
O
+
O
Monte Carlo (MC) events, corresponding to inclusive
inelastic collisions without a bias toward a specific hard process,
modeled with the HIJING v1.3b event generator
[
63
]
.
The generated events were passed through a full simulation of the ATLAS
detector
[
64
]
using
Geant4

[
65
]
,
and reconstructed using the same algorithms which are applied to the data.
Three alternative MC samples were produced using geometry variations that
contain additional material in either the IBL, the remainder of the ID, or the
pixel-detector services in the forward region.
These are used to
assess systematic uncertainties in the measurement that result from
the limited accuracy of the detector-material model
used in the simulation.

4
Analysis

The measurements presented in this paper include:

i

computation of the charged-particle
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
(or
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
) and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(\eta)

(or
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(y_{\pi})
) in the fiducial range of the analysis, i.e.
0.27<p_{\text{T}}<5

GeV
and
|\eta|<2.5
(or
|y_{\pi}|<2.25
);

ii

measurement of the invariant
4
4

4

Here “invariant” refers to invariance under longitudinal boosts.
Strictly speaking, only the
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y
distribution is invariant, whereas
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
and
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
are not.

double-differential per-event charged-particle yields,

\displaystyle Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})\equiv\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}n}{p_{\text{T}}\,\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})\equiv\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}n}{p_{\text{T}}\,\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}},

(1)

iii

extrapolation of the yields in Eq. (
1
) to
p_{\text{T}}=0\penalty\ \text{GeV}
by fitting the invariant
yields to smooth functions that are integrated analytically or numerically,
and estimation of the total or “extrapolated”
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
(or
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
)
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(\eta)
(or
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(y_{\pi}))
.

The measurements performed using
y_{\pi}
provide important physics
results as well as an essential calibration of the distortion of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra that results from the use of pseudorapidity. That distortion
is demonstrated using the HIJING Monte Carlo sample in
Section
4.2
, and is accounted for when performing
the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation of the
\eta
-based measurements using the
ratio of the measured
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
and
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
distributions.

4.1
Fiducial measurements

In this analysis, primary charged particles are those with a mean lifetime
greater than
0.3\times 10^{-10}
s, produced either directly in the nucleus–nucleus
interaction or in decays of particles with shorter lifetimes
[
66
]
.
For primary charged particles, the efficiency (
\varepsilon
) of the track reconstruction procedure
and subsequent selections is determined using the HIJING MC sample.
The same sample is used to determine the misreconstructed-track
5
5

5

These are tracks
that do not correspond to true charged particles but arise
from combinatorial hits in the detector.

(“fake-track”) fraction (
f_{\text{fake}}
) and the secondary-track fraction
(
f_{\text{sec}}
) among the selected tracks.
The latter arise mainly from weak decays of hadrons or interactions
with detector material.
The
\varepsilon
,
f_{\text{fake}}
, and
f_{\text{sec}}
values are obtained after using standard
ATLAS procedures
[
51
,
54
]
to match reconstructed
tracks to primary charged particles generated by HIJING.
Because of potential sensitivity of the reconstruction to detector occupancy,
the
\varepsilon
,
f_{\text{fake}}
, and
f_{\text{fake}}
values are determined separately in
intervals of reconstructed charged-particle multiplicity
N_{\text{chg}}^{\text{rec}}
computed over
|\eta|<2.5
and

0.4<p_{\text{T}}<5

GeV
.
6
6

6

A lower bound is placed on

p_{\text{T}}
in the multiplicity calculation to match
the definition in previous ATLAS measurements
[
37
]
.

The use of multiplicity instead of centrality allows the same corrections
to be applied to
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
data and avoids potential complications
in attempting to match HIJING and
O
+
O
or
Ne
+
Ne
centralities.

When evaluating
\varepsilon
,
f_{\text{fake}}
, and
f_{\text{sec}}
, per-particle weights are applied
to MC events to correct for
p_{\text{T}}
-spectrum differences between data
and simulation and to adjust the kaon and proton fractions of the
total particle yield as a function of
p_{\text{T}}
to match measurements by
ALICE in
Xe
+
Xe
collisions
[
67
]
.
These “flavor weights” are applied because the tracking efficiency depends
on the particle-species composition.
The flavor reweighting is performed in a centrality-independent manner,
using the ALICE measurements of the kaon-to-pion and proton-to-pion
ratios in the 50–60%
Xe
+
Xe
centrality interval to correct the
corresponding HIJING fractions.
The 50–60% centrality
Xe
+
Xe
collisions are chosen because the multiplicity
in this interval roughly corresponds to that in 0–10% centrality

O
+
O
collisions.
To evaluate the systematic uncertainties associated with the flavor reweighting,
alternative weights derived from the 70–90% and 40–50%
Xe
+
Xe
centrality
intervals measured by ALICE are also applied.
The reweighting factor depends on
p_{\text{T}}
and is
\sim
1–1.6 for kaons
and
\sim
0.5–1.4 for protons.
To account for higher secondary-track rates due to enhanced
strange hadron production
[
67
]
, a
p_{\text{T}}
-independent
weight of 1.5, motivated by the maximum reweighting factor for kaons,
is applied to secondary tracks from
K_{\text{s}}
,
\Lambda
,
and
\Sigma
decays.

A data-driven procedure is used to verify the fake-track fraction and
improve the secondary-track fraction estimates obtained from the MC simulation.
This procedure takes advantage of the fact that the distributions of
distance of closest approach of a track to the beamline,
d_{0}
,
are observed in MC events to have significantly different shapes for
reconstructed primary particles, fake tracks, and secondary tracks,
especially at low
p_{\text{T}}
where the fake-track fractions are largest.
Template fits to measured
d_{0}
distributions are performed in three
\eta
intervals and in the
p_{\text{T}}
bins used in the analysis.
These yield
f_{\text{fake}}
values within a few tenths of a percent of those obtained
from simulation, but
f_{\text{sec}}
values larger than those obtained
from simulation. The ratios of the template-fit and MC-evaluated
secondary-track rates obtained in the three
\eta
intervals are then
applied as per-secondary-particle weights in the simulation. These weights
vary from near unity at low
p_{\text{T}}
to a maximum value between 2 and 2.5 near

p_{\text{T}}=2

GeV
, and then decrease with increasing
p_{\text{T}}
.
No significant centrality dependence is observed in the template-fit
results, so the applied secondary-track reweighting factors are
the same for all
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
centralities.
Separate weights are obtained for the nominal and alternative track selections.
The systematic uncertainty associated with secondary particles is estimated
from the difference between results obtained with the two selections, each
using its own set of weights.

In the data analysis, a per-track correction weight that accounts
for efficiency, fake tracks, and secondary particles is computed
according to

w^{\text{corr}}=\frac{1-(f_{\text{fake}}+f_{\text{sec}})}{\varepsilon}.

(2)

Example results for the reconstruction efficiency
\varepsilon
and the combined
fake- and secondary-track fraction
f_{\text{fake}}+f_{\text{sec}}
, obtained for the
nominal track-selection working point are shown in Figure
1
as
functions of
p_{\text{T}}
for two
\eta
intervals and two
N_{\text{chg}}^{\text{rec}}
intervals.
Figure
1
also shows the bin-by-bin correction factor

C_{\text{bbb}}
, which is discussed later and corrects for
migration of the reconstructed tracks in
p_{\text{T}}
and
\eta
.
The reconstruction efficiency increases significantly with increasing

p_{\text{T}}
at low
p_{\text{T}}
, but increases slowly with
p_{\text{T}}
for
p_{\text{T}}\gtrsim 0.7

GeV
.
The combined fake- and secondary-track rates fall rapidly
with increasing
p_{\text{T}}
at low
p_{\text{T}}
, primarily due to the
p_{\text{T}}
dependence
of the fake-track fractions.
At higher
p_{\text{T}}
, the
f_{\text{fake}}+f_{\text{sec}}
value is dominated by secondary tracks,
whose fraction varies slowly with
p_{\text{T}}
.

Figure 1:

Example (a, b) reconstruction efficiencies,
\varepsilon
, and bin-by-bin
correction factors,
C_{\mathrm{bbb}}
, together with (c, d)
fake- and secondary-track fractions,
f_{\text{fake}}+f_{\text{sec}}
.
The plots correspond to the nominal track-selection working
point and are shown for multiplicity intervals (a, c)

50<N_{\text{chg}}^{\text{rec}}<140
and (b, d)
300<N_{\text{chg}}^{\text{rec}}<700
,
and for pseudorapidity intervals
|\eta|<1.0
and
2.0<|\eta|<2.5
.

Aside from the effects of reconstruction efficiency, fake tracks,
and secondary tracks, the measurements are affected by migration of
the measured tracks in
p_{\text{T}}
and
\eta
.
The
p_{\text{T}}
resolution of the ID is dominated by multiple scattering
in the
p_{\text{T}}
range of the measurement, so
\sigma_{p_{\text{T}}}/p_{\text{T}}

varies slowly with
p_{\text{T}}
.
For
|\eta|<1
,
\sigma_{p_{\text{T}}}/p_{\text{T}}
decreases from 3% at
p_{\text{T}}=0.2

GeV
to 2% at 0.4
GeV
and is nearly constant for higher
p_{\text{T}}
.
At the most forward pseudorapidities (
|\eta|>2
),
\sigma_{p_{\text{T}}}/p_{\text{T}}
decreases
from
{\sim}6\%
at
p_{\text{T}}=0.2

GeV
to 5% at
p_{\text{T}}=0.4

GeV
,
above which it remains constant.
The (absolute)
\eta
resolution,
\sigma_{\eta}
, decreases with
increasing
p_{\text{T}}
.
For
|\eta|<1
,
\sigma_{\eta}
decreases from 0.012 at
p_{\text{T}}=0.2

GeV
to 0.002 at
p_{\text{T}}=5

GeV
, while for
2<|\eta|<2.5
it decreases from
0.018 at
p_{\text{T}}=0.2

GeV
to 0.002 at
p_{\text{T}}=5

GeV
.
Given the quoted
p_{\text{T}}
and
\eta
resolutions and the chosen
p_{\text{T}}
and

\eta
intervals for the measurement, bin-by-bin corrections for
migration in
p_{\text{T}}
and
\eta
are sufficient for the measurement.
Thus, instead of applying the corrections in Eq. (
2
)
and unfolding the measurements, simple bin-by-bin correction weights

w^{\text{bbb}}=\frac{1-(f_{\text{fake}}+f_{\text{sec}})}{C_{\text{bbb}}}

are applied to each particle in the data analysis.
Here,
C_{\text{bbb}}
is the bin-by-bin correction factor
that accounts for both reconstruction efficiency and
migration in
p_{\text{T}}
and
\eta
, and is evaluated as a
function of
p_{\text{T}}
,
\eta
, and
N_{\text{chg}}^{\text{rec}}
.
Example results for
C_{\text{bbb}}
are shown in
Figure
1
.

The measured
\eta
-dependent quantities are defined for the fiducial region
in the following equations:

\frac{{\mathrm{d}n_{\text{fid}}^{\text{cent}}}}{\mathrm{d}\eta}\equiv\frac{1}{N^{\text{cent}}_{\text{evt}}}\dfrac{\sum\limits_{i\in\text{cent}}\sum\limits_{j\in\Delta\eta}w^{\text{bbb}}_{j}}{\Delta\eta}\quad\text{and}\quad\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle_{\text{fid}}^{\text{cent}}\equiv\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in\text{cent}}\sum\limits_{j\in\Delta\eta}{p_{\text{T}}}_{j}\,w^{\text{bbb}}_{j}}{\sum\limits_{i\in\text{cent}}\sum\limits_{j\in\Delta\eta}w^{\text{bbb}}_{j}},

where the labels “cent” and “fid” indicate the centrality interval
and the fiducial region of the measurement, the
index
i
runs over events satisfying the event and
centrality selections, and the index
j
runs over particles
within a given
\Delta\eta
interval. Similar expressions with
\eta
replaced by
y_{\pi}
define
the
y_{\pi}
-dependent observables
The double-differential invariant yields are computed according to

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}{n}}{p_{\text{T}}\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}\mathrm{d}\eta}\equiv\frac{1}{N^{\text{cent}}_{\text{evt}}}\displaystyle\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in\text{cent}}\sum\limits_{j\in(\Delta\eta,\Delta p_{\text{T}})}\frac{w^{\text{bbb}}_{j}}{{p_{\mathrm{T}}}_{j}}}{\Delta\eta\Delta p_{\text{T}}}.

(3)

The measurements use 20 linear intervals and 36 logarithmic intervals in
\eta
and
p_{\text{T}}
, respectively, spanning the fiducial ranges.
The measurements as a function of
y_{\pi}
use the same intervals as

\eta
, but results are not reported in the first and last interval,
owing to the loss of
y_{\pi}
acceptance.

Applications of the above-described analysis procedure to
the reconstructed HIJING MC sample show closure of the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
,
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
,
and double-differential yields to
{\ll}1
% except in the 0–5%
centrality interval, where the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
yield exhibits a

{\sim}0.5\%
degree of
\eta
-dependent nonclosure.
This nonclosure arises from the finite multiplicity binning of the
correction factors and is included in the systematic uncertainties
of the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
measurements.

4.2
Rapidity variables and impact on measurements

The physics of particle production in hadronic or nuclear collisions
is most naturally expressed in terms of particle
rapidity (
y
).
The difference between
\eta
and
y
depends on
p_{\text{T}}
such that

y
values migrate “outward”, to larger
|\eta|
values, at
low
p_{\text{T}}
, with the migration larger for increasing particle masses.
The migration of particles away from mid-rapidity leads to the well-known
depletion near

\eta\sim 0
in measurements of
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
in symmetric hadron–hadron
or nucleus–nucleus collisions. Since the migration is largest at
low
p_{\text{T}}
, it also has a significant impact on
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
.
Because most of the produced particles in hadronic collisions are
pions, the use of
y_{\pi}
as an alternative to
\eta
removes much of
the distortion present in
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(\eta)
.

Figure 2:

Comparison of (a)
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
,
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
, and
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y
and
(b)
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
computed in intervals of
\eta
,
y_{\pi}
, and
y
obtained from stable charged particles
with
|y|<2.7
and
0<p_{\text{T}}<5

GeV
generated by
HIJING (circles) or EPOS (diamonds) in events satisfying the 0–5%
O
+
O
centrality
selection. The bottom panels show (a) the ratio
of
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
to
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y
and (b) the difference between

\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(\eta)
or
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(y_{\pi})
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(y)
.

Figure
2
demonstrates the above considerations
and the impact of the differences among the three rapidity variables,

\eta
,
y_{\pi}
, and
y
, based on comparisons using the HIJING sample and
a sample generated with the EPOS event generator
[
68
]
.
The figure compares
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
,
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
and
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y

for primary charged particles with
0<p_{\text{T}}<5\penalty\ \text{GeV}
,
produced by the generators in 5.36
TeV

O
+
O
collisions in the 0–5% centrality interval.
Comparisons between the
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
values as a function of
\eta
,
y_{\pi}
and
y
are also shown.
As the figure demonstrates,
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
is
smaller than
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y
everywhere, but especially so near
\eta=0
. The
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
values are also smaller than
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y
, but the maximum
reduction is only
{\sim}5\%
compared to a
{\sim}20\%
reduction
in
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
. The
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
evaluated as a function of
\eta
is
significantly higher – by as much as 50
MeV
– than that
evaluated using true rapidity owing to the
migration of low-
p_{\text{T}}
particles away from
\eta\sim 0
.
However, the
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
evaluated as a function
of
y_{\pi}
is much closer to
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle(y)
, differing by less than 5
MeV
.
These observations justify the use of
y_{\pi}
for some of the
measurements presented in this paper.

Figure 3:

Ratios of invariant differential
p_{\text{T}}
spectra:
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
/
Y_{y}(p_{\text{T}})
(blue),

Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
/
Y_{y}(p_{\text{T}})
(red), and
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
/
Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
(green)
for primary charged particles generated in 5.36
TeV

O
+
O
collisions by (a, b, c) HIJING and (d, e, f) EPOS.
Results are shown for three different intervals of
\eta
,
y_{\pi}
, or
y
,
with the interval varying from left to right.
The dashed vertical line indicates
p_{\text{T}}=0.27

GeV
,
the nominal minimum
p_{\text{T}}
selection used in this analysis.

The impact of using different rapidity variables when evaluating the
invariant double-differential yields is illustrated in
Figure
3
, which show ratios of
(i)
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
to
Y_{y}(p_{\text{T}})\equiv(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y
,
(ii)
Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
to
Y_{y}(p_{\text{T}})
, and
(iii)
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
to
Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
.
Results are shown for HIJING at the top and EPOS at the bottom for three intervals of
\eta
,

y_{\pi}
, or
y
.
The effect of the migration in
\eta
can be clearly
observed at low
p_{\text{T}}
, where the
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
distribution is strongly
suppressed relative to
Y_{y}(p_{\text{T}})
. The
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
-to-
Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
ratio shows a similar suppression at low
p_{\text{T}}
, which
means that the pions – or the effects of the small pion mass –
dominate the migration responsible for the suppression
of
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
at low
p_{\text{T}}
. That conclusion has important
implications for this measurement, namely that the bulk of the distortion of

Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
can be obtained from the data by using the
measured
ratios

R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})=\frac{Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})}{Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})}\equiv\frac{(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta}{(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}},

(4)

where the numerator and denominator are evaluated using the
intervals of
\eta
or
y_{\pi}
.
The residual distortion of the
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
spectra, not included
in
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
, is contained in the ratio

R_{y_{\pi},y}(p_{\text{T}})=\frac{Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})}{Y_{y}(p_{\text{T}})}\equiv\frac{(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}}{(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y},

which shows a maximum deviation from unity of about 7%.

The distortion of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra seen in
Figure
3
must be accounted for when
performing the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation. Fortunately, the bulk of that
distortion can be evaluated using the
measured

R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
, which
greatly reduces potential sensitivity of the measurement to
mismodeling of the data – particularly the flavor distributions – in
the HIJING MC simulation.

4.3
Extrapolation to zero transverse momentum

As described in the introduction, in addition to measuring

\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
within the fiducial acceptance, this measurement
also includes an extrapolation to
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
.
That extrapolation is performed by fitting the
p_{\text{T}}
dependence of

Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
or
Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
to a function that can be integrated either
analytically or numerically.
Several functions, (
G(p_{\text{T}})
) based on either the Hagedorn function

\displaystyle G^{\text{H}}(p_{\text{T}})

\displaystyle\equiv A/\left(1+\frac{p_{\text{T}}}{p_{0}}\right)^{n},

(5)

with
A
,
p_{0}
and
n
being fit parameters,
or the Tsallis form
G^{\text{T}}(p_{\text{T}})
, in which
p_{\text{T}}
in Eq. (
5
)
is replaced by the transverse mass
m_{\mathrm{T}}=\sqrt{p_{\text{T}}^{2}+m_{\pi}^{2}}
,
calculated under the charged-pion mass hypothesis,
were considered as part of the analysis.
The Hagedorn function was originally proposed
[
69
]

because it could interpolate between negative exponential behavior at
low
p_{\text{T}}
or
m_{\mathrm{T}}
and power-law evolution at high
p_{\text{T}}
.
Even so, neither the Hagedorn nor Tsallis function provides an adequate
description of the data.
However, augmenting the Hagedorn and Tsallis functions with an
additional negative exponential (NE) term in
p_{\text{T}}
or
m_{\mathrm{T}}
, respectively:

\displaystyle G^{\text{H+NE}}(p_{\text{T}})

\displaystyle\equiv A/\left(1+\frac{p_{\text{T}}}{p_{0}}\right)^{n}+Be^{-p_{\text{T}}/T},\,\,\text{and}\,\,G^{\text{T+NE}}(p_{\text{T}})\equiv A/\left(1+\frac{m_{\mathrm{T}}}{p_{0}}\right)^{n}+Be^{-m_{\mathrm{T}}/T},

(6)

where
B
and
T
are additional fit-parameters, provides a
significantly better fit to the data.
Among the studied parameterizations,
7
7

7

“Blast-wave”
fits
[
70
]
were also considered but did not
provide an improved description of the data.

these two forms provide the best description of the data over the
full
p_{\text{T}}
range of the measurement.
Although the Tsallis plus negative exponential function,

G^{\text{T+NE}}
, yields better closure when applied to the HIJING-
and EPOS-generated primary charged-particle distributions in
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions, the
G^{\text{H+NE}}

function provides better fits to the data, so it is used as the nominal model for
extrapolating to
p_{\mathrm{T}}=0
, while results obtained with

G^{\text{T+NE}}
are used for evaluating systematic uncertainties.

Fits to Eq. (
6
) are used to extrapolate the
Y_{y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
distributions
to
p_{\mathrm{T}}=0
.
However, in order to properly fit the above functions to the
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
distributions, the modification of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra resulting from the
migration between
y
and
\eta
illustrated in
Figure
3
must be addressed. As suggested
above, nearly all of the modification can be measured using

R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
. Figure
4
shows results
for this ratio in
O
+
O
collisions in the 0–5% centrality interval in
two
|\eta|
intervals. The ratios are evaluated over an extended
p_{\text{T}}
range,
0.21<p_{\text{T}}<5

GeV
in order to best constrain
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
at
lower
p_{\text{T}}
.
The results are compared with similar ratios constructed using
primary charged particles generated by HIJING (red circles) and EPOS (green diamonds).
HIJING describes the measured
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
in both
|\eta|
intervals.
EPOS describes the data well for
|\eta|<0.25
but
systematically deviates from the measured
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
at
larger
|\eta|
, as evidenced by the small differences between EPOS
and data in the
1<|\eta|<1.25
interval.

Figure 4:

Results for
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
(Eq. (
4
)) in data
compared with results from HIJING (red circles) and EPOS (green diamonds)
in the 0–5% centrality interval.
Results are shown for (a)
|\eta|,|y_{\pi}|<0.25
and
(b)
1<|\eta|,|y_{\pi}|<1.25
.

The variations of the measured
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
with
p_{\text{T}}
are
well described by functions of the form

R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}^{\text{fit}}(p_{\text{T}})=\frac{h(p_{\text{T}})}{C+h(p_{\text{T}})},\penalty\ \text{with}\penalty\ h(p_{\text{T}})=\left(p_{\text{T}}\right)^{a}\left[1+\gamma\left(p_{\text{T}}\right)^{a}\right],

(7)

where
C
,
a
and
\gamma
are fit parameters.
The values of the parameterized functions in Eq. (
7
) decrease
as
p_{\text{T}}\rightarrow 0
and approach unity at large
p_{\text{T}}
.
The data cannot constrain the term involving
\gamma
in Eq. (
7
), but the MC

R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}^{\text{fit}}(p_{\text{T}})
distributions, evaluated
over
0<p_{\text{T}}<5

GeV
, are better described with a small, nonzero
value for
\gamma
that is used when fitting the data.
Resulting fits to the measured
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}(p_{\text{T}})
are shown with
the curves in Figure
4
.
The ratios of the MC
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
values to those obtained from the data
are shown by the curves in the lower panels.
The incorporation of the MC-determined
\gamma
term in
h(p_{\text{T}})

ensures that
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}^{\text{fit}}(p_{\text{T}})
remains
compatible with both HIJING and EPOS at low
p_{\text{T}}
, except at larger

|\eta|
, where EPOS shows a systematic deviation from the data.
In order to assess the potential bias resulting from the use of MC simulation
to constrain
\gamma
, the analysis is repeated with
\gamma=0
,
and the difference is included as a systematic uncertainty.

The parameter

C
in Eq. (
7
) is small and consistent with zero at large

|\eta|
and is largest at
\eta=0
. For all centrality
intervals in both
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions, the
\eta
dependence of
C

is well described by a Gaussian function centered at zero. In the application
of
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}^{\text{fit}}(p_{\text{T}})
in the analysis described
below, Gaussian fits to
C(\eta)
are performed separately for each
collision system, centrality interval, and analysis variation, and the
results of the fit are used when evaluating

R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}^{\text{fit}}(p_{\text{T}})
. Statistical variations of the
Gaussian fit functions at the 68% confidence level are used to assess systematic
uncertainties associated with the smoothing of
C(\eta)
.

Figure 5:

Measured
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
for a subset of the
\eta
intervals used in the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and

\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
measurement for
O
+
O
(left) and
Ne
+
Ne
(right) collisions in the 0–1% (top)
and 60–70% (bottom) centrality intervals. The spectra are each scaled by a factor of
2^{n}
,
as indicated in the legend, to improve visibility between data measured in different
\eta
intervals. Statistical errors are shown but are smaller than the size of the data points.
The solid lines show the results of fits to the data using the expression in
Eq. (
8
) with
G=G^{\text{H+NE}}
.

The complete parameterization of the
p_{\text{T}}
dependence of
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
is
expressed as

f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})=R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{fit}}(p_{\text{T}})\,\times R_{y_{\pi},y}(p_{\text{T}})\,\times G^{\text{model}}(p_{\text{T}}),

(8)

where
G^{\text{model}}(p_{\text{T}})
is either
G^{\text{H+NE}}
or
G^{\text{T+NE}}
.
This function, with
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}^{\mathrm{fit}}
obtained from fits to data (Eq. (
7
))
and
R_{y_{\pi},y}
obtained from MC simulation, is fitted to the measured data in each
\eta
interval
using both
G^{\text{H+NE}}(p_{\text{T}})
and
G^{\text{T+NE}}(p_{\text{T}})
.
The nominal fits are performed over the transverse momentum range
0.27<p_{\text{T}}<5

GeV
.
Results of such fits are illustrated in
Figure
5
, which shows the
measured
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
distributions versus
p_{\text{T}}
in a subset of the
analyzed
\eta
intervals for
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions in the 0–1%
and 60–70% centrality intervals. The fits obtained using Eq. (
8
)
with the
G^{\text{H+NE}}
functional form are also shown. The fits
are capable of describing the full shape of the
measured
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
, although small deviations of the fit from the data would not
be visible in the figure. To better illustrate the quality of the fits,
Figure
6
shows results for
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
in
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions in 0–5% and 70–80% centrality
intervals using “symmetrized” pseudorapidity intervals
|\eta|<0.25
(top) and
2.0<|\eta|<2.25
(bottom) and ratios of the
fit function to the data. The ratios differ
from unity by
{<}2\%
at low
p_{\text{T}}
, but show a systematic deviation
from the data above 3 GeV. That deviation, however, has negligible
impact on the extrapolated
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
results.

Figure 6:

Measured
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
and their ratios to fit functions (see
Eq. (
8
)) in the pseudorapidity intervals
(a, b)
|\eta|<0.25
and (c, d)
2.0<|\eta|<2.25

for (a, c)
O
+
O
collisions and (b, d)
Ne
+
Ne
collisions in
the 0–5% and 70–80% centrality intervals. Solid red and
dashed black lines in each panel represent fits of the functions

G^{\text{H+NE}}(p_{\text{T}})
and
G^{\text{T+NE}}(p_{\text{T}})
, respectively, to the data.
The error bars on the data points indicate statistical uncertainties only.
The dotted horizontal lines in the lower section of each panel
indicate unity and unity
\pm 5\%
.

While the extrapolated
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
can, in principle, be computed
directly from
f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
, for the purposes of this
measurement the focus is on correcting the fiducial measurements.
Thus, instead, the fit results are used to compute the two quantities

\displaystyle F_{\text{extr}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{0}^{5\penalty\ \text{GeV}}{\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}}\;p_{\text{T}}\,f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}}{\int_{0.27\penalty\ \text{GeV}}^{5\penalty\ \text{GeV}}{\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}}\;p_{\text{T}}\,f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\Delta_{\text{extr}}=\frac{\int_{0}^{5\penalty\ \text{GeV}}\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}\;p_{\text{T}}^{2}\,f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}}{\int_{0}^{5\penalty\ \text{GeV}}\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}\;p_{\text{T}}\,f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}}-\frac{\int_{0.27\penalty\ \text{GeV}}^{5\penalty\ \text{GeV}}\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}\;p_{\text{T}}^{2}\,f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}}{\int_{0.27\penalty\ \text{GeV}}^{5\penalty\ \text{GeV}}\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}\;p_{\text{T}}\,f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}}.

(9)

where the
p_{\text{T}}
factors in the integral are needed to convert from
invariant differential yields to, e.g.,
\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\text{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
.
The quantity
F_{\text{extr}}
represents the multiplicative factor needed to
correct the fiducial
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
to the full, extrapolated
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
,
while
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
is an additive correction needed to convert the
fiducial
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
to the full extrapolated result. In both
F_{\text{extr}}
and
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
, the normalization of
f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}
cancels out in the ratio. Systematic
variations of
F_{\text{extr}}
and
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
associated with the extrapolation
procedure are made by including the MC-motivated
\gamma
term in

h(p_{\text{T}})
(see Eq. (
7
)) or not, by varying the
a
parameter within statistical
uncertainties, by varying
C(\eta)
within
the uncertainty in the Gaussian smoothing function as described above, and by including
the
R_{y_{\pi},y}(p_{\text{T}})
obtained from HIJING or setting that term to unity
in Eq. (
8
). These variations are found to
change the
F_{\text{extr}}
values by at most a few tenths of a percent and

\Delta_{\text{extr}}
by less than a few MeV. The inclusion of
R_{\eta,y_{\pi}}
in
Eq. (
8
) reduces
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
F_{\text{extr}}
by
an
\eta
-dependent fraction that at
\eta=0
is
{\lesssim}5\%
and
increases
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
and
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
by a
\eta
-dependent shift that is

{\lesssim}25

MeV
.

Because
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collision systems are intrinsically
forward-backward symmetric, the corrections to the fiducial
measurements of
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
, the
F_{\text{extr}}
and
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
values,
should be symmetric in rapidity.
To ensure that symmetry, and to remove statistical fluctuations,

F_{\text{extr}}
and
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
are fitted to even polynomial functions of
\eta
.
However, the data with
|\eta|>2.25
are excluded from the
smoothing procedure because the tracking performance is poorer
at forward rapidities, leading to larger variations in

F_{\text{extr}}
and
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
that are not well described by
the even-polynomial fits.
Examples of the results for
F_{\text{extr}}
and
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
obtained from the
aforementioned procedures, and the fits obtained to smooth the
values, are shown in Figure
7
.

Figure 7:

Example results for (a, c)
F_{\text{extr}}
and (b, d)
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
obtained by
fitting measured
Y_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
distributions to the form in
Eq. (
8
) and applying Eqs. (
9
). Results are shown for the (a, b) 0–1% and (c, d) 55–60%
centrality intervals in
O
+
O
collisions.
The solid lines show the fits used to smooth and ensure that

F_{\text{extr}}
and
\Delta_{\text{extr}}
are symmetric around
\eta=0
.
The data with
|\eta|>2.25
are excluded from the
smoothing, as the tracking is poorer at forward rapidities.

5
Systematic uncertainties

Systematic uncertainties in the measurements
arise primarily from potential inaccuracies in the corrections for
reconstruction efficiency, fake- and secondary-track rates, and bin migration.
Such effects can originate from mismodeling of the detector geometry
and response in the MC simulations, imperfect modeling of the true particle kinematic
distributions in
O
+
O
or
Ne
+
Ne
collisions, differences between the detector
conditions during data-taking and those assumed in the
MC simulation, or from the procedures used to evaluate and apply
the corrections.
In addition to MC-based effects, the measurements can be affected by
uncertainties in the definition of the centrality intervals, biases
associated with the trigger selection, or background events passing
the event selection.
For results extrapolated to
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
, additional uncertainties arise
from the modeling of the extrapolation procedure.
The evaluation of the systematic uncertainties associated with these
sources is described below.

i

Track selection:

The track selection criteria determine the relative contributions of
primary charged particles, secondary particles, and fake tracks
entering the analysis.
The results obtained with the nominal selection are compared with
those using the alternative track selections described in
Section
3
.
By varying the tracking requirements, the relative contributions of primary,
secondary, and fake tracks are modified, and the associated systematic
uncertainty therefore covers the likely spread of the contributions
from secondary and fake tracks in the data.
This uncertainty varies only weakly with centrality and is taken to
be symmetric about the nominal measurement.

ii

Centrality definition:

The uncertainty in the centrality determination over the 0–80% centrality range is
estimated to be
\pm 2.2\%
(
\pm 2.5\%
) for the
O
+
O
(
Ne
+
Ne
) data.
This uncertainty reflects variations in the nuclear density profiles
used in the Glauber fits, as well as additional sources such as
changes to the
\Sigma E_{\text{T}}^{\text{FCAL}}
thresholds above which the fits are performed.
The systematic uncertainty associated with the centrality definition
is evaluated by repeating the full analysis using alternative
centrality thresholds obtained from the Glauber study.
This uncertainty exhibits a strong dependence on centrality: it is
smallest in the most central collisions and increases
monotonically for increasingly peripheral collisions.
For the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
measurements, this is the dominant systematic
uncertainty affecting the most peripheral centrality intervals.

iii

Tracking efficiency:

As mentioned in Section
4
, the measurements
use corrections to adjust the tracking efficiencies and
fake- and secondary-track rates that are obtained using MC simulations.
The sensitivity of the results to the modeling of the detector
material and geometry is evaluated using previously described alternative MC samples.
The difference between the results obtained with the alternative and
nominal simulations is taken as the systematic uncertainty.
The point-to-point variations are assumed to be symmetric about the
nominal result and are combined in quadrature.
The resulting fractional uncertainties are largely independent of
centrality, but show a strong dependence on
\eta
for the

\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
measurement.
At forward pseudorapidity (
|\eta|>2
), this source becomes one of
the dominant contributors to the total uncertainty in
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
.

iv

MC reweighting:

As described in Section
4
, residual mismodeling of
the particle kinematic distributions in HIJING is addressed through

p_{\text{T}}
-dependent and flavor-dependent reweighting of the MC-generated
charged-particle distributions.
The reweighting factors are obtained from comparisons with ALICE measurements of

K/\pi
and
p/\pi

p_{\text{T}}
spectra in
Xe
+
Xe
collisions
[
67
]
,
and are applied to kaons and protons in the HIJING simulation.
The nominal analysis uses reweighting factors obtained from the
50–60% centrality interval in
Xe
+
Xe
collisions.
This
Xe
+
Xe
multiplicity roughly corresponds to 0–10%
centrality
O
+
O
collisions.
Alternative reweighting
factors are derived from the 40–50% and 60–90% centrality intervals,
and the resulting changes in the measured observables are included
as a systematic uncertainty.

v

MC nonclosure:

A closure test is performed by comparing the values of
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and

\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
obtained directly from generated particles in the MC
simulation with those obtained by applying the full reconstruction
and analysis procedure to the simulated events.
Closure is found to hold within statistical uncertainties over
the 5–80% centrality range.
However, for the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
measurement in the most central collisions,
a small degree of nonclosure is observed.
This nonclosure amounts to approximately 1% in the 0–1%
centrality interval.
A systematic uncertainty is therefore assigned to the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
measurement; it is zero above 5% centrality and increases
linearly from 0% at 4–5% centrality to 1% at 0–1% centrality.
No nonclosure effect is observed for the
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
measurement, and no
corresponding systematic uncertainty is assigned.

vi

Fit model for
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation:

This uncertainty applies only to results extrapolated to
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
.
It accounts for the dependence of the extrapolated measurements on
the functional form used to describe the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra.
As discussed in Section
4
the nominal results
are obtained using a “Hagedorn + negative exponential” parameterization.
The change in the results when using an alternative
“Tsallis + negative exponential” parameterization is assigned as a
systematic uncertainty.
This uncertainty is taken to be symmetric about the nominal value.
It is one of the leading uncertainties in the results after extrapolation to
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
.

vii

Fit range for
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation:

This uncertainty also applies only to results extrapolated to
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
.
It accounts for the dependence of the extrapolation on the
p_{\text{T}}
range used in the fit.
The nominal extrapolation uses fits to the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra over the
range 0.27–5.0
GeV
.
Alternative fit ranges of 0.24–5.0
GeV
and 0.27–3.9
GeV
are
used to assess this uncertainty.

viii

y_{\pi}{-}\eta
weight:

This uncertainty is another that applies only to results extrapolated to
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
.
The value of
R_{\eta,y{\pi}}^{\text{fit}}(p_{\text{T}})
(Eq. (
7
))
is varied within its uncertainties, and the effect is propagated
through the analysis.
This includes setting of the
\gamma
term in Eq. (
7
) to zero,
as discussed in Section
4.3
.
Additionally, checks are done where the MC-determined
R_{y_{\pi},y}
term
is removed from
f^{\text{fit}}_{\eta}(p_{\text{T}})
(Eq. (
8
))
and the analysis is repeated.
The change in the final results is found to be negligible.

Figure 8:

Summary of the systematic uncertainty estimates for (a, b)
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and (c, d)
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
in
O
+
O
collisions, for the (a, c) 0–1%
and (b, d) 60–70% centrality intervals.
Both the total uncertainty and its components are shown.

and (b)

at mid-rapidity (
|\eta|<0.5
), as a function
of centrality in

collisions.

Ne
+

to
O
+

measurements
for (c)
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and (d)
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
are also shown.
The plots show the total uncertainty and the contributions from
the individual components.

The total systematic uncertainty is obtained as the quadrature sum
of the individual contributions.
Figure
8
summarizes the systematic
uncertainties for the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
measurements in
O
+
O
collisions for a central and a peripheral centrality interval.
The uncertainty associated with the chosen functional form of the
fit model is typically the leading systematic uncertainty.
However, in peripheral collisions, the uncertainty from the
centrality determination also contributes significantly.
Figure
9
shows the centrality dependence
of the systematic uncertainties at mid-rapidity.
Figure
9
also shows the systematic
uncertainties in the
Ne
+
Ne
/
O
+
O
ratios of the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
spectra,
where correlated uncertainties cancel out.
Uncertainties not associated with the extrapolation also apply to the

p_{\text{T}}>0.27

GeV
fiducial measurements.

6
Results

This section presents a subset of the results
– in most cases for a limited set of centrality intervals – of the measurements performed
using the procedures described in the preceding sections.
Results not included here are presented in the appendix.

6.1
Theoretical calculations

The measurements presented in this paper are compared with theoretical
calculations from Ref.
[
71
]

(labeled as “IPGlasma”) and from Ref.
[
31
]

(labeled as “Trento”). They are also compared with events generated with the
EPOS model
[
68
]
. The following paragraphs describe
each calculation in more detail.

The “IPGlasma” calculation combines the Projected Generator Coordinate
Method (PGCM)
[
72
]
, an
ab initio
nuclear-structure
model for oxygen and neon, with the IPGlasma framework
[
73
]

to compute the initial energy densities of the
colliding nuclei.
These energy densities are evolved using viscous relativistic
hydrodynamics implemented in MUSIC
[
6
]
.
A hadronic afterburner based on UrQMD
[
74
,
75
]

is subsequently applied to obtain the final particle spectra
and multiplicities.
The estimated theory uncertainties in the IPGlasma calculations are
purely statistical.

The “Trento” calculations are described in Ref.
[
31
]
,
and use either the PGCM
[
72
]
or the Nuclear Lattice Effective
Field Theory (NLEFT) simulations
[
72
,
61
]
to determine
the nuclear configurations of oxygen and neon.
These configurations are used to generate initial-state energy
densities using the Trento model
[
35
]
.
The resulting events are evolved using the Trajectum hydrodynamic
framework
[
76
,
62
]
to obtain the final particle
spectra and multiplicity distributions.
The estimated theory uncertainties in the Trento calculations include statistical uncertainties
from finite event sampling and systematic uncertainties obtained by
sampling multiple parameter sets from a Bayesian posterior constrained
by
Pb
+
Pb
data for several observables (see Refs.
[
31
,
77
]
),
as well as variations arising from finite grid spacing and nuclear-structure modeling.

The EPOS calculations are performed using version 4.0.3 of the EPOS model in its
core–corona configuration
[
78
,
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
]
including hydrodynamic modeling.
Initial particle production is described within a parton-based
Gribov–Regge multiple scattering framework, with parton ladders
hadronizing via string fragmentation.
The produced matter is separated into a dense core, which undergoes
hydrodynamic evolution, and a dilute corona, which hadronizes
without significant final-state interactions.
Final-state hadronic rescatterings are included through a hadronic cascade.
The estimated theory uncertainties in the EPOS calculations are
purely statistical.

In the figures shown in this section, the statistical and
systematic uncertainties of the Trento model calculations
are combined in quadrature and shown as total uncertainties
for clarity.
Since the IPGlasma and EPOS model calculations include only
statistical uncertainties, their displayed total uncertainties
are purely statistical.

The Trento calculations are available only for the

p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolated case, and are not shown when
comparing with the
p_{\text{T}}>270\penalty\ \text{MeV}
fiducial measurements,
except when comparing ratios where the effect of the fiducial
acceptance largely cancels.
Similarly, IPGlasma calculations for the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra are
not available in 1%-wide centrality intervals and are not
shown when comparing with the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra measured in 1%-wide
centrality intervals.

6.2
Transverse momentum distributions

Figure 10:

The measured
p_{\text{T}}
spectra in (a, c, e)
O
+
O
and
(b, d, f)
Ne
+
Ne
collisions at mid-rapidity
for the (a, b) 0–1%, (c, d) 20–25%
and (e, f) 50–55% centrality intervals.
For comparison, predictions from models described in Ref.
[
71
]

(labeled “IPGlasma”) and Ref.
[
31
]
(labeled “Trento”),
as well as the EPOS model, are also shown.
For the data, the vertical lines and shaded bars indicate statistical and
systematic uncertainties, respectively, and in most cases are smaller
than the size of the data points.
For the theory calculations, the shaded bands indicate total uncertainties.

Figure
10
presents the
double-differential invariant yields,
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
, measured in
O
+
O
(left) and
Ne
+
Ne
(right) collisions in three centrality intervals and
also shows the
corresponding theoretical distributions.
Figure
11

presents the ratios of the theory predictions to the data.
Figures
28
–
29

and
30
–
31

in the Appendix respectively show invariant yields and ratios of theory predictions to data
in additional centrality intervals.
In general, the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra in the data are qualitatively reproduced by the
theory calculations, especially at low
p_{\text{T}}
.
For
p_{\text{T}}<2

GeV
, the Trento calculations are within about
\pm 15
% of the data,
but show a systematic deviation with increasing
p_{\text{T}}
that is more pronounced in the more
peripheral collisions.
In contrast, the IPGlasma and EPOS ratios to data are nearly

p_{\text{T}}
-independent and within about
\pm 5
% of unity for
p_{\text{T}}\lesssim 1
GeV.
Both the Trento and IPGlasma calculations underestimate the data for

p_{\text{T}}\gtrsim 2
GeV, as does EPOS but to a lesser degree.
The failure of the Trento and IPGlasma calculations to describe the
data at high
p_{\text{T}}
presumably arises because the hydrodynamic models
do not include hard processes. Hard processes are included in EPOS,
but the hard-scattering rates may be insufficient to describe the data.

Ratios of the theory predictions to the measured
p_{\text{T}}
spectra in (a, c, e)
O
+

and
(b, d, f)
Ne
+

collisions at mid-rapidity
for the (a, b) 0–1%, (c, d) 20–25%
and (e, f) 50–55% centrality intervals.
Ratios based on predictions from models described in

[

]
(labeled “IPGlasma”)
and Ref.
[

]
(labeled “Trento”),
as well as the EPOS model, are shown.
The shaded bands indicate total uncertainties.

Figure 12:

The ratio of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra measured in
Ne
+
Ne
and
O
+
O
collisions
at mid-rapidity (
|\eta|<0.5
),
for the (a) 0–1%, (b) 20–25%, (c) 25–40%, and (d) 50–55%
centrality intervals.
Comparisons with calculations from models described in
Ref.
[

]
(labeled “IPGlasma”)
and Ref.
[

]
(labeled “Trento”),
as well as the EPOS model, are also shown.
For the data, the vertical lines and shaded bars indicate statistical and

For the theory points, the shaded bands indicate total uncertainties.

Figure
12
shows the ratio of the

Ne
+
Ne
to
O
+
O

p_{\text{T}}
spectra, together with comparisons with
theoretical calculations.
Figure
32

in the Appendix shows similar results for additional
centrality intervals.
Clear differences between data and the
theoretical predictions are seen in the magnitude of the ratios,
indicating deficiencies in the description of the (relative)
overall particle yield. These are
discussed further below. The measured
Ne
+
Ne
to
O
+
O
ratios are found
to be nearly independent of
p_{\text{T}}
, with the ratios varying by
{<}10\%
over the measured range.
However, in the more central collisions – i.e. in the top panels –
a slow decrease in the ratio with increasing
p_{\text{T}}
is seen,
while in the more peripheral intervals – bottom panels –
a slight increase in the ratio with increasing
p_{\text{T}}
is observed at
low
p_{\text{T}}
. The EPOS calculations generally reproduce the trends seen in the
measurements, except in the 20–25% centrality interval. In contrast,
the IPGlasma- and Trento-based hydrodynamic model calculations
exhibit a much stronger
p_{\text{T}}
dependence in the
Ne
+
Ne
to
O
+
O
ratios
than is observed in the data, with the NLEFT and PGCM Trento
calculations showing opposite trends. In central collisions, the data
lie within the range spanned by the NLEFT and PGCM Trento predictions,
suggesting that neither of the Trento models provides the correct initial
conditions for the hydrodynamic evolution.
In the most peripheral centrality interval shown in the figure,
the hydrodynamic calculations disagree with the measurements
but exhibit a weak
p_{\text{T}}
dependence similar to that observed
in the data.

.

shows the ratios of the

p_{\text{T}}
-integrated multiplicity
in the corresponding
\eta
or
y_{\pi}
interval.
This normalization removes the overall change in particle production
between the centrality intervals, allowing the ratios to emphasize changes
in the shape of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra.
Figures

and

in the Appendix show similar results for additional
centrality intervals in
O
+
O
collisions.
Figures
35
and
36

in the Appendix show similar results for
Ne
+
Ne
collisions.
From these ratios, it is observed that when going from peripheral to central
collisions, the spectra become
progressively harder, with the yields below
p_{\text{T}}\sim 0.5
GeV
suppressed in the more central collisions, and the yields at higher
p_{\text{T}}
enhanced. However, the enhancement then decreases with increasing
p_{\text{T}}
such
that by 5 GeV the ratios have little centrality dependence.
This trend is observed in both the
\eta
-binned measurements and the
y_{\pi}
-binned
measurements, and at both mid-rapidity and forward rapidity.
The observed behavior is consistent with the development of stronger radial
flow in the more central
O
+
O
collisions combined with the dominance of
hard processes at the upper end of the measured
p_{\text{T}}
range.

To more directly test the rapidity dependence of the hardening
of the
p_{\text{T}}
spectra, Figure
14

shows the ratios of the
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
(left) and
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
(right)
spectra at 0–1% and 30–40% centrality to those at 70–80% centrality at mid-rapidity
(
|\eta,y_{\pi}|<0.5
) and forward rapidity (
1.5<|\eta,y_{\pi}|<2.5
).
Results for additional centrality intervals are shown in the Appendix,
in Figures
37
–
38
for
O
+
O
collisions
and in Figures
39
–
40
for
Ne
+
Ne
collisions.
The
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
and
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
ratios are
consistent, indicating that the bias in the
\eta
distributions at low

p_{\text{T}}
cancels out in the ratios. The mid-rapidity and forward-rapidity
ratios are consistent within uncertainties,
indicating that the radial flow developed in the
O
+
O
collisions is
empirically observed to be approximately boost-invariant over
|\eta|<2.5
.

Figure
15
compares the
measured
(1/p_{\mathrm{T}})\;\!\mathrm{d}^{2}n/\mathrm{d}p_{\mathrm{T}}\,\mathrm{d}\eta
ratios with those obtained from the model calculations.
Figures
41

and
42

in the Appendix show similar results for additional centrality
intervals for
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions, respectively.
In general, the ratios in the model calculations show a stronger
dependence on
p_{\text{T}}
than observed in the data.
The EPOS ratios reach a maximum and then decrease
with increasing
p_{\text{T}}
as in the data, but the maximum values are substantially
higher than in the data except in the most peripheral (65–70%)
centrality interval.
The hydrodynamic calculations keep increasing monotonically,
probably because of the lack of hard contributions.

Results of the
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
and
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}y_{\pi}
measurements are shown in
Figure
16
for
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions in several representative centrality intervals.
Results are presented for the fiducial region
p_{\text{T}}>270\penalty\ \text{MeV}

as well as for the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation.
Results for additional centrality intervals are presented
in Figures
43
–
46

in the Appendix.
The
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
distributions exhibit the expected dependence
on pseudorapidity.
A dip is observed at mid-pseudorapidity, which arises from
presenting the distributions as a function of pseudorapidity
rather than rapidity (see Section
4.2
).
This dip is more pronounced for the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation and
is largely removed when the measurements are plotted as a
function of
y_{\pi}
.
The
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
measurements as a function of
\eta
and
y_{\pi}
are
shown in Figure
17
for both
the
p_{\text{T}}>270\penalty\ \text{MeV}
fiducial region and the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation.
Results for additional centrality intervals are presented
in Figures
47
–
50

in the Appendix.
The
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
values peak at mid-(pseudo)rapidity and decrease
toward forward rapidities.
In addition,
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
increases monotonically when going from peripheral
to central collisions.
The
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
values obtained from the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation are
approximately 1.4 times larger than the corresponding
fiducial measurements.
In contrast, the
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
values from the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation
are approximately 150–200
MeV
lower than the fiducial measurements,
reflecting the inclusion of low-
p_{\text{T}}
particles in the
extrapolated spectra.
Across the measured
\eta
range, the
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
variation is

\sim
100
MeV
for the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
case.
In comparison, the
y_{\pi}
-dependent measurements shown in
Figures
17
(e) and
17
(f)
exhibit a much weaker dependence, varying by only
\sim
30
MeV
over the measured
y_{\pi}
range.
As discussed previously, this difference arises from the
kinematic mapping between rapidity and pseudorapidity.
Particles at a given
|y_{\pi}|
correspond to larger
|\eta|
values,
with the migration being more pronounced at low
p_{\text{T}}
.
Consequently, low-
p_{\text{T}}
particles shift further outward when
plotted versus
\eta
, which increases
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
and reduces

\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
near
|\eta|\sim 0
, leading to the stronger variation
in the results plotted as a function of
\eta
.
This effect is clearly shown in Figure
18
,
where the
\eta
- and
y_{\pi}
-dependent measurements are plotted together.
Similar comparisons for additional centrality intervals,
as well as for the
p_{\text{T}}>0.27

GeV
fiducial measurements,
are included in the Appendix in Figures
51
–
54
for
O
+
O
collisions,
and in Figures
55
–
58
for
Ne
+
Ne
collisions.

.

6.4
Centrality dependence of particle yields and mean
transverse momenta

Figure 21:

The centrality dependence of
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
measured at mid-rapidity
(

) in (a)

+

and (b)
Ne
+
Ne
collisions.
Comparisons with calculations from models described in
Ref.
[
71
]
(labeled “IPGlasma”) and
Ref.
[
31
]
(labeled “Trento”) as well as the
EPOS model are also shown.
Theory-to-data ratios are shown for (c)
O
+
O
and (d)
Ne
+
Ne
collisions.
For the data in panels (a, b), the vertical lines and shaded bars
indicate statistical and systematic uncertainties, respectively,
and in all cases are smaller than the size of the data points.
For the theory points in panels (a, b) and the theory-to-data ratios
in panels (c, d), the shaded bands indicate combined
(statistical + systematic) uncertainties.

Figure
21
shows the centrality
dependence of
\mathrm{d}n/\mathrm{d}\eta
measured at mid-rapidity
(
|\eta|<0.5
) for the
p_{\mathrm{T}}>0\penalty\ \text{MeV}
extrapolation.
Comparisons with model calculations are also included.
As is typical in nucleus–nucleus collisions, the charged-particle
multiplicity rises most steeply toward the most central collisions.
Overall, the models reproduce the observed centrality dependence
reasonably well.
For both the
O
+
O
and
Ne
+
Ne
collisions, the Trento-based hydrodynamic
calculations agree with the measurements within
\sim
1
\sigma

uncertainties over the 0–60% centrality range.
The IPGlasma-based hydrodynamic calculations and the EPOS model
show even better agreement with the data.
This is illustrated by the theory-to-data ratios shown in the
lower panels of Figure
21
.
In peripheral collisions, noticeable discrepancies between
data and the Trento- and IPGlasma-based model calculations emerge.
These differences are larger for
O
+
O
than for
Ne
+
Ne
collisions, and
are more pronounced for the Trento model than for the IPGlasma model.
Part of the discrepancy may arise from the different rapidity regions
used to define centrality in the data and in the Trento-
and IPGlasma-based hydrodynamic model calculations.
In the data, centrality is determined using forward-calorimeter
transverse energy.
For the EPOS model comparisons, the centrality was determined using
the generated final particles in the FCal acceptance.
However, the Trento and IPGlasma based model calculations shown here
determine centrality by using charged-particle multiplicity at mid-rapidity.
Similar comparisons for the
p_{\text{T}}>0.27

GeV
fiducial region are
shown in Figure
63
in the Appendix.
The fiducial measurements show similar trends.

of the data points.
For the theory points in panels (a, b) and the theory-to-data ratios
in panels (c, d), the shaded bands indicate combined
(statistical + systematic) uncertainties.

Figure

shows the centrality

\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle

values are nearly
identical for
O
+

and
Ne
+

collisions.
At a given centrality, the differences between the two systems are
within 0.5% (less than 5
MeV
) over the full measured centrality range.
The
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
decreases monotonically from central to peripheral
collisions, with a total variation of
\sim
100
MeV
across the centrality range.
The model calculations reproduce the observed centrality dependence
well and typically agree with the data within
\pm 5\%
.
The Trento-based hydrodynamic calculations predict
\langle p_{\mathrm{T}}\rangle
values
slightly higher than those observed in the data.
The EPOS model calculations transition from higher values than in data in
central collisions to lower values in peripheral
collisions.
The IPGlasma-based hydrodynamic calculations agree best
with the measurements.

p_{\text{T}}>0.27

GeV
fiducial region are
shown in Figure

in the Appendix.
The fiducial measurements show similar trends.
